Geodesic Convexity of the Symmetric Eigenvalue Problem and Convergence of Steepest Descent
Authors: Foivos Alimisis, Bart Vandereycken
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
We study the convergence of the Riemannian steepest descent algorithm on the Grassmann manifold for minimizing the block version of the Rayleigh quotient of a symmetric matrix. Even though this problem is non-convex in the Euclidean sense and only very locally convex in the Riemannian sense, we discover a structure for this problem that is similar to geodesic strong convexity, namely, weak-strong convexity. This allows us to apply similar arguments from convex optimization when studying the convergence of the steepest descent algorithm but with initialization conditions that do not depend on the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0, we prove exponential convergence rates, while otherwise the convergence is algebraic. Additionally, we prove that this problem is geodesically convex in a neighbourhood of the global minimizer of radius \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {O}}(\sqrt{\delta })$$\end{document}O(δ).

Introduction

We consider the problem of computing the top k eigenvectors of a symmetric matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times n}$$\end{document}A∈Rn×n, which has many applications in numerical linear algebra (low rank approximation), statistics (principal component analysis) and signal processing. Without loss of generality, we assume that A is also positive semidefinite. This is because A can be shifted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A+c I_n$$\end{document}A+cIn for some constant c and this transformation does not change its eigenvectors.

We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1 \ge \lambda _2 \ge \cdots \ge \lambda _n$$\end{document}λ1≥λ2≥⋯≥λn the eigenvalues of A counted with multiplicity and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta :=\lambda _k-\lambda _{k+1}$$\end{document}δ:=λk-λk+1 the eigengap for some k between 1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-1$$\end{document}n-1. We also denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{\alpha } = \text {diag}(\lambda _1, \ldots ,\lambda _k)$$\end{document}Λα=diag(λ1,…,λk) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{\beta } = \text {diag}(\lambda _{k+1}, \ldots ,\lambda _n)$$\end{document}Λβ=diag(λk+1,…,λn).

A set of k leading eigenvectors of A can be found by minimizing the function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(X) = -{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(X^TAX) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)=-Tr(XTAX)over the set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \times k$$\end{document}n×k matrices with orthonormal columns. Indeed, from Fan’s trace minimization theorem (see, e.g., [18, Corollary 4.3.39]) we know that1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min \{ f(X) :X \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times k}, X^T X = I_k \} = -(\lambda _1 + \cdots + \lambda _k)=-{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\Lambda _{\alpha })=:f^*. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}min{f(X):X∈Rn×k,XTX=Ik}=-(λ1+⋯+λk)=-Tr(Λα)=:f∗.Since A is symmetric, we can define the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\alpha } = \begin{bmatrix} v_1 \hspace{2mm} \cdots \hspace{2mm} v_k \end{bmatrix}$$\end{document}Vα=v1⋯vk such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\alpha }^T V_{\alpha } = I_k$$\end{document}VαTVα=Ik and with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_i \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}vi∈Rn a unit-norm eigenvector corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i$$\end{document}λi. If the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ is strictly positive, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(V_{\alpha })$$\end{document}span(Vα) is unique; otherwise, we can choose any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_k$$\end{document}vk from a subspace with dimension equal to the multiplicity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _k$$\end{document}λk. It is readily seen that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(V_{\alpha })= -(\lambda _1 + \cdots + \lambda _k)$$\end{document}f(Vα)=-(λ1+⋯+λk). In fact, all minimizers of (1) are of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\alpha } Q$$\end{document}VαQ with Q a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \times k$$\end{document}k×k orthogonal matrix. We also define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\beta }=\begin{bmatrix} v_{k+1} \hspace{2mm} \cdots \hspace{2mm} v_n \end{bmatrix}$$\end{document}Vβ=vk+1⋯vn that contains the eigenvectors corresponding to the eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{k+1},\ldots ,\lambda _n$$\end{document}λk+1,…,λn. Its columns span the orthogonal complement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(V_{\alpha })$$\end{document}span(Vα) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\beta }^T V_{\beta } = I_{n-k}$$\end{document}VβTVβ=In-k and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\alpha }^T V_{\beta } = {0}_{k \times (n-k)}$$\end{document}VαTVβ=0k×(n-k).

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(V_{\alpha }) = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(V_{\alpha }Q)$$\end{document}span(Vα)=span(VαQ), it is more natural to consider this problem as a minimization problem on the Grassmann manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k), the set of k-dimensional subspaces in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn. Let us therefore redefine the objective function as2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X}) = -{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(X^TAX) \text { where}\,\mathcal {X} = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(X) \hbox { for }X \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times k}\hbox { s.t. }X^T X = I_k. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)=-Tr(XTAX)whereX=span(X)forX∈Rn×ks.t.XTX=Ik.This cost function can be seen as a block version of the standard Rayleigh quotient. An immediate benefit is that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta > 0$$\end{document}δ>0, the minimizer of (2) is isolated since it is the subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V_{\alpha }} = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(V_\alpha )$$\end{document}Vα=span(Vα).

To minimize f on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k), we shall use the Riemannian steepest descent method (RSD) along geodesics in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k). Quite remarkably, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k) these geodesics can be implemented efficiently in closed form.

For analyzing the convergence properties of steepest descent on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k), we extend results of the recent work [4], where it is shown that the Rayleigh quotient on the sphere enjoys favourable geodesic convexity-like properties, namely, weak-quasi-convexity and quadratic growth. In this work, we show that these convexity-like properties continue to hold in the more general case of the block Rayleigh quotient function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k) \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:Gr(n,k)→R. These results are of general interest, but also sufficient to prove a local convergence rate for steepest descent for minimizing f when started from an initial point outside the region of local convexity. For the latter, a crucial help is provided by the fact that the Grassmann manifold is non-negatively curved (see [33]).

In particular, assuming a strictly positive eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _k$$\end{document}λk and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{k+1}$$\end{document}λk+1, we prove an exponential convergence rate to the subspace spanned by the k leading eigenvectors, similar to the convergence of power method and subspace iteration (Theorem 5.1). If we do not assume any knowledge regarding the eigengap, then we can still prove a sub-exponential (polynomial) convergence rate of the function values to the global minimum (Theorem 5.2), but we cannot directly study the convergence to a global minimizer. This is in line with previous work but our analysis does not use standard notions of geodesic convexity and allows for an initial guess further from the global minimizer. In Appendix B we present related convergence results for steepest descent with a more tractable step size but at the expense of needing a slightly better initialization.

Related Work

The symmetric eigenvalue problem has been popular for several decades in the numerical linear algebra and optimization communities. When only a few eigenvalues are targeted, the main solvers for this problem have been based on subspace iteration and Krylov subspace methods. Less but still considerable attention has been given to the steepest descent method and its accelerated versions. Most works on steepest descent focus only on computing the first leading eigenvector of a symmetric matrix (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1$$\end{document}k=1), using a Euclidean version of the algorithm. Asymptotic convergence rates are known for this setting since the 1950’s, see [15]. More recently, exact non-asymptotic estimates for the same Euclidean steepest descent with exact line search were proved in [20]. For a more comprehensive overview of this line of research, the reader can refer to [26] and the references therein. A recent result that takes a different route compared to the previous ones is [4]. There, a steepest descent algorithm on the sphere is analyzed using newly proved convexity-like properties of the spherical Rayleigh quotient.

Regarding the block version of the algorithm, where one targets multiple pairs of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, much less is known. We refer here to [27], which presents a steepest descent-like method for the multiple eigenvector problem using Ritz projections onto a 2k-dimensional subspace in each step. The convergence of this algorithm is proved to be linear, but computing the Ritz projections is quite expensive. Instead, in this work we consider a much cheaper version of steepest descent by directly choosing only one of the vectors in this 2k-dimensional subspace to update our algorithm. Some analysis for such a steepest descent (without Ritz projection) on the Grassmann manifold using a retraction and an Armijo step-size is provided in [2] (see Algorithm 3 and Theorem 4.9.1). Unfortunately this convergence rate is asymptotic, that is, a linear rate is achieved after an unknown number of iterations. The region in which the convergence happens cannot be quantified. Also, such a convergence rate does not yield an iteration complexity for the algorithm.

The optimization landscape provided by the block Rayleigh quotient on the Grassmann manifold has also received some attention lately. [32] provides many interesting properties of the critical points of this function and proves that all but the global optimum are strict saddles. This is later used to derive favourable convergence properties for a hybrid method consisting of Riemannian steepest descent in a first stage and a Riemannian Newton’s method in a final stage. [23] proves the so-called robust strict saddle property for this function, that is, the Hessian evaluated in each critical point except the global optimum has both positive and negative eigenvalues in a whole neighborhood. However, none of these papers talks about (generalized) convexity of any form, nor discusses any convergence rates for steepest descent.

Turning the discussion to the convexity properties of eigenvalue problems, there is a new line of research concerned by that. In [34], the authors prove (Theorem 4) that the Rayleigh quotient is geodesically gradient dominated in the sphere (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1$$\end{document}k=1), that is, it satisfies a spherical version of the Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequality. In [4], it is shown that this result of [34] can be strengthened to a geodesic weak-quasi-convexity and quadratic growth property, which imply gradient dominance when combined. Finally, the recent paper [3] examines (among other contributions) the convexity structure of the same block version of the symmetric eigenvalue problem on the Grassmann manifold that we introduced above. Unfortunately, the characterization of the geodesic convexity region independently of the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ (Corollary 5 in [3]) is wrong (see our Appendix A for a counterexample). As we will prove in Theorem A.1, the geodesic convexity region of f (and the one of the equivalent cost function used in [3]) needs to depend on the eigengap, as appears also in [19, Lemma 7] in the case of the sphere (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1$$\end{document}k=1).

To the best of our knowledge, the current work is the first that provides non-asymptotic convergence rates for the steepest descent algorithm for the multiple eigenvalue-eigenvector problem on the Grassmann manifold. We mainly rely on the work [4], which proves exponential convergence of steepest descent only in the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1$$\end{document}k=1, that is, for the leading eigenvector. In this paper, we take a reasonable but highly non-trivial step forward by extending the convexity-like characterization of the spherical Rayleigh quotient to general k, that is, for a block of k leading eigenvectors. Again, the paper [9] is of high value for our current work regarding weakly-strongly-convex functions.

As mentioned above, the standard algorithm for computing the leading eigenspace of dimension k is subspace iteration (or power method when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1$$\end{document}k=1).1 However, there are reasons to believe that, in certain cases, Riemannian steepest descent (and its accelerated version with non-linear conjugate gradients) should be preferred, especially in noisy settings [4] or in electronic structure calculations where the leading eigenspace of many varying matrices A needs to be computed.2 In particular, [4] presents strong experimental evidence that steepest descent is more robust to perturbations of the matrix–vector products than subspace iteration close to the optimum. While subspace iteration still behaves better at the start of the iteration, it asymptotically fails to converge to an approximation of the leading subspace that is as good as the one estimated by Riemannian steepest descent. While [4] dealt with a noisy situation due to calculations in a distributed setting with limited communication, exactly the same effect can be observed when we inject the matrix–vector products with Gaussian noise. Thus, we expect steepest descent to perform better than subspace iteration close to the optimum in any stochastic regime [14].

Regarding worst-case theoretical guarantees, the strongest convergence result for subspace iteration in the presence of a strictly positive eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ is in terms of the largest principal angle between the iterates and the optimum [13], that is, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _\infty $$\end{document}ℓ∞-norm of the vector of principal angles. In contrast, our convergence result for steepest descent for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta > 0$$\end{document}δ>0 (Theorem 5.1) is in terms of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ2-norm of the same vector of angles, which is in general stronger. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta = 0$$\end{document}δ=0, it is known from [21, 28] that the largest eigenvalue (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1$$\end{document}k=1) can still be efficiently estimated. We extend this result for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k>1$$\end{document}k>1 and prove a convergence rate of steepest descent for the function values f (Theorem 5.2), relying only on weak-quasi-convexity (and thus using a different argument from [21, 28]).

Geometry of the Grassmann Manifold and Block Rayleigh Quotient

We present here a brief introduction into the geometry of the Grassmann manifold. The content is not new and for more details, we refer to [2, 7, 12].

The (n, k)-Grassmann manifold is defined as the set of all k-dimensional subspaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)=\lbrace \mathcal {X} \subseteq \mathbb {R}^n :\mathcal {X} \hspace{1mm} \text {is a subspace and} \dim (\mathcal {X})=k \rbrace . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gr(n,k)={X⊆Rn:Xis a subspace anddim(X)=k}.Any element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k) can be represented by a matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times k}$$\end{document}X∈Rn×k that satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X} = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(X)$$\end{document}X=span(X). Such a representative is not unique since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y=XQ$$\end{document}Y=XQ for some invertible matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q \in \mathbb {R}^{k \times k}$$\end{document}Q∈Rk×k satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(Y) = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(X)$$\end{document}span(Y)=span(X). Without loss of generality, we will therefore always take matrix representatives X of subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X that have orthonormal columns throughout the paper. With some care, the non-uniqueness of the representatives is not a problem.3 For example, our objective function (2) is invariant to Q.

Riemannian structure. The set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k) admits the structure of a differential manifold with tangent spaces3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_{\mathcal {X}} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)=\lbrace G \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times k} :X^T G=0 \rbrace , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TXGr(n,k)={G∈Rn×k:XTG=0},where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X} = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(X)$$\end{document}X=span(X). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T G = 0$$\end{document}XTG=0 if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(XQ)^T G=0$$\end{document}(XQ)TG=0, for any invertible matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q \in \mathbb {R}^{k \times k}$$\end{document}Q∈Rk×k, this description of the tangent space does not depend on the representative X. However, a specific tangent vector G will depend on the chosen X. With slight abuse of notation,4 the above definition should therefore be interpreted as: given a fixed X, we define tangent vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1, G_2, \ldots $$\end{document}G1,G2,… of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k) at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(X)$$\end{document}X=span(X).

This subtlety is important, for example, when defining an inner product on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathcal {X}} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}TXGr(n,k):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle G_1, G_2 \rangle _{\mathcal {X}} = {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(G^T_1 G_2) \ \text {\ with}\,G_1,G_2 \in T_{\mathcal {X}} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨G1,G2⟩X=Tr(G1TG2)\ withG1,G2∈TXGr(n,k).Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1$$\end{document}G1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_2$$\end{document}G2 are tangent vectors of the same representative X. Observe that the inner product is invariant to the choice of orthonormal representative: If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{G}_1=G_1 Q$$\end{document}G¯1=G1Q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{G}_2 = G_2 Q$$\end{document}G¯2=G2Q with orthogonal Q, then we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \bar{G}_1, \bar{G}_2 \rangle _{\mathcal {X}} = {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\bar{G}^T_1 \bar{G}_2) = {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(Q^T G_1^T G_2 Q)= {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(G_1^T G_2 Q Q^T) = {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(G_1^T G_2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨G¯1,G¯2⟩X=Tr(G¯1TG¯2)=Tr(QTG1TG2Q)=Tr(G1TG2QQT)=Tr(G1TG2).It is easy to see that the norm induced by this inner product in any tangent space is the Frobenius norm, which we will denote throughout the paper as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert :=\Vert \cdot \Vert _F$$\end{document}‖·‖:=‖·‖F.

Exponential map. Given the Riemannian structure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k), we can compute the exponential map at a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X as [1, Thm. 3.6]4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Exp}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}: T_{\mathcal {X}} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)&\rightarrow {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k) \\ G&\mapsto {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(\, X V \cos (\Sigma ) + U \sin (\Sigma ) \, ), \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ExpX:TXGr(n,k)→Gr(n,k)G↦span(XVcos(Σ)+Usin(Σ)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U \Sigma V^T$$\end{document}UΣVT is the compact SVD of G such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma $$\end{document}Σ and V are square matrices.

The exponential map is invertible in the domain [7, Prop. 5.1]5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ G \in T_{\mathcal {X}} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k) :\Vert G \Vert _2 < \frac{\pi }{2} \right\} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G∈TXGr(n,k):‖G‖2<π2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert G \Vert _2$$\end{document}‖G‖2 is the spectral norm of G. The inverse of the exponential map restricted to this domain is the logarithmic map, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}$$\end{document}Log. Given two subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Y}\in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X,Y∈Gr(n,k), we have6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {Y}) = U {{\,\textrm{atan}\,}}(\widehat{\Sigma }) \, V^T, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LogX(Y)=Uatan(Σ^)VT,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \widehat{\Sigma }V^T = (I - X X^T) Y (X^T Y)^{-1}$$\end{document}UΣ^VT=(I-XXT)Y(XTY)-1 is again a compact SVD. This is well-defined if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T Y$$\end{document}XTY is invertible, which is guaranteed if all principal angles between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}Y are strictly less than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi / 2$$\end{document}π/2 (see below). By taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G = {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}G=LogX(Y), we see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma = {{\,\textrm{atan}\,}}(\widehat{\Sigma })$$\end{document}Σ=atan(Σ^).

Principal angles. The Riemannian structure of the Grassmann manifold can be conveniently described by the notion of the principal angles between subspaces. Given two subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Y} \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X,Y∈Gr(n,k), the principal angles between them are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \theta _1 \le \cdots \le \theta _k \le \pi /2$$\end{document}0≤θ1≤⋯≤θk≤π/2 obtained from the SVD7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y^T X=U_1 \cos \theta \ V_1^T \end{aligned}$$\end{document}YTX=U1cosθV1Twhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1 \in \mathbb {R}^{k \times k}, V_1 \in \mathbb {R}^{k \times k}$$\end{document}U1∈Rk×k,V1∈Rk×k are orthogonal and the diagonal matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cos \theta = {{\,\textrm{diag}\,}}(\cos \theta _1,...,\cos \theta _k)$$\end{document}cosθ=diag(cosθ1,...,cosθk). Notice that the definition of principal angles is indeed independent of the specific orthonormal representatives of the subspaces in question.

We can express the Riemannian logarithm using principal angles and the intrinsic distance induced by the Riemannian inner product discussed above is8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Y)}=\Vert {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}} (\mathcal {Y}) \Vert = \Vert {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {Y}} (\mathcal {X}) \Vert =\sqrt{\theta _1^2+...+\theta _k^2}=\Vert \theta \Vert _2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dist(X,Y)=‖LogX(Y)‖=‖LogY(X)‖=θ12+...+θk2=‖θ‖2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta =(\theta _1, \ldots ,\theta _k)^T$$\end{document}θ=(θ1,…,θk)T. For more details on these facts, the reader can refer to section 4.3 in [12] (arc length distance).

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times k}$$\end{document}X∈Rn×k is an arbitrary matrix with orthonormal columns, then, generically, these columns will not be exactly orthogonal to the k leading eigenvectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1, \ldots , v_k$$\end{document}v1,…,vk of A. Thus, we have with probability one that the principal angles between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and the space of k leading eigenvectors satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \theta _1 \le \cdots \le \theta _k < \pi /2$$\end{document}0≤θ1≤⋯≤θk<π/2.

Curvature. We can compute exactly the sectional curvatures in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k), but for our purposes we only need that they are everywhere non-negative [7, 33]. This means that the geodesics on the Grassmann manifold spread more slowly than in Euclidean space. This is consequence of the famous Toponogov’s theorem (see [11]) that we state here in the form of the following technical lemma, which will be important in our convergence analysis.

Lemma 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}, \mathcal {Y}, \mathcal {Z} \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X,Y,Z∈Gr(n,k), such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\max \{ \text {dist}(\mathcal {X}, \mathcal {Z}), \text {dist} (\mathcal {Y}, \mathcal {Z}) \} < \frac{\pi }{2}. $$\end{document}max{dist(X,Z),dist(Y,Z)}<π2.Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {dist}(\mathcal {X}, \mathcal {Y}) \le \Vert {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_\mathcal {Z}(\mathcal {X})-{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_\mathcal {Z}(\mathcal {Y}) \Vert . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dist(X,Y)≤‖LogZ(X)-LogZ(Y)‖.

Lemma 3.2

(Law of cosines) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Y},\mathcal {Z}$$\end{document}X,Y,Z as in Lemma 3.1. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X}, \mathcal {Y}) \le {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {Z}, \mathcal {X})+ {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {Z}, \mathcal {Y})-2 \langle {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {Z}} (\mathcal {X}), {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {Z}} (\mathcal {Y}) \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dist2(X,Y)≤dist2(Z,X)+dist2(Z,Y)-2⟨LogZ(X),LogZ(Y)⟩.

Proof

Apply Lemma 3.1 and expand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_\mathcal {Z}(\mathcal {X})-{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_\mathcal {Z}(\mathcal {Y}) \Vert ^2$$\end{document}‖LogZ(X)-LogZ(Y)‖2. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Block Rayleigh quotient. Our objective function for minimization is the block version of the Rayleigh quotient:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X}) = -{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(X^TAX) \text { where}\,\mathcal {X} = {{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(X) \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)\hbox { s.t. }X^T X = I_k. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)=-Tr(XTAX)whereX=span(X)∈Gr(n,k)s.t.XTX=Ik.This function has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }={{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(\begin{bmatrix} v_1 \hspace{2mm} \cdots \hspace{2mm} v_k \end{bmatrix})$$\end{document}Vα=span(v1⋯vk) as global minimizer. This minimizer is unique on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0.

Given any differentiable function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k) \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:Gr(n,k)→R, we can define its Riemannian gradient as the vector field that satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} df(\mathcal {X})(G) = \langle {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}), G \rangle _{\mathcal {X}}, \hspace{1mm} \text {for any} \hspace{1mm} G \in T_{\mathcal {X}} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}df(X)(G)=⟨gradf(X),G⟩X,for anyG∈TXGr(n,k).For a given representative X of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X, the Riemannian gradient of the block Rayleigh quotient satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}) = -2(I-X X^T) A X. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gradf(X)=-2(I-XXT)AX.Using the notions of the Riemannian gradient and Levi-Civita connection, we can define also a Riemannian notion of Hessian. For the block Rayleigh quotient f, the Riemannian Hessian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Hess}\,}}f$$\end{document}Hessf evaluated as bilinear form satisfies9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Hess}\,}}f(\mathcal {X})[{G},{G}] = 2 \langle G, G X^T A X - A G \rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hessf(X)[G,G]=2⟨G,GXTAX-AG⟩,for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G \in T_{\mathcal {X}} {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}G∈TXGr(n,k); see [12, §4.4] or [2, §6.4.2].

Convexity-like Properties of the Block Rayleigh quotient

We now prove the new analytic properties of the block Rayleigh quotient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\mathcal {X})=-{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(X^T A X)$$\end{document}f(X)=-Tr(XTAX). These are important in their own right but will also be used later for the convergence of the Riemannian steepest descent method.

Smoothness

A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 function defined on the Grassmann manifold is called \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ-smooth if the maximum eigenvalue of its Riemannian Hessian is everywhere upper bounded by a positive constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ. This is true for the block Rayleigh quotient, as we show in the next proposition:

Proposition 1

(Smoothness) The eigenvalues of the Riemannian Hessian of f on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k) are upper bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma := 2 (\lambda _1 - \lambda _n)$$\end{document}γ:=2(λ1-λn).

Proof

Let G be a tangent vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Gr(n,k) at X. Then the Riemannian Hessian satisfies (see (9))\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{2} {{\,\textrm{Hess}\,}}f(\mathcal {X})[G,G] = {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(G^T G X^T A X) - {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(A G G^T). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}12Hessf(X)[G,G]=Tr(GTGXTAX)-Tr(AGGT).Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A, X^T A X, G G^T$$\end{document}A,XTAX,GGT, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^T G$$\end{document}GTG are all symmetric and positive semi-definite matrices, standard trace inequality (see, e.g, [18, Thm. 4.3.53]) gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Hess}\,}}f(\mathcal {X})[G,G] \le 2 (\lambda _{\max }(X^T A X)- \lambda _{\min } (A)) \Vert G \Vert ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hessf(X)[G,G]≤2(λmax(XTAX)-λmin(A))‖G‖2.Since X has orthonormal columns, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{\max }(X^T A X) \le \lambda _{\max }( A )$$\end{document}λmax(XTAX)≤λmax(A); see, e.g., [18, Cor. 4.3.37]. The proof is now complete with the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1$$\end{document}λ1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _n$$\end{document}λn. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The result in Prop. 1 is tight: Choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X = V_\alpha $$\end{document}X=Vα and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G = v_n e_1^T$$\end{document}G=vne1T, it is readily verified that the upper bound is attained. From now on, we refer to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ as the specific value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2(\lambda _1-\lambda _n)$$\end{document}2(λ1-λn). This value also features in a useful upper bound for the spectral norm of the gradient. This bound is independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X:

Lemma 4.1

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X} \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X∈Gr(n,k) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =2(\lambda _1-\lambda _n)$$\end{document}γ=2(λ1-λn), the Riemannian gradient of f satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}) \Vert _2 \le \frac{\gamma }{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖gradf(X)‖2≤γ2.

Proof

Since X has orthonormal columns, we can complete it to the orthogonal matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q = \begin{bmatrix} X&X_\perp \end{bmatrix}$$\end{document}Q=XX⊥. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}) \Vert _2 = \Vert 2 (I - XX^T)AX \Vert _2 = 2 \Vert X_\perp ^T A X \Vert _2$$\end{document}‖gradf(X)‖2=‖2(I-XXT)AX‖2=2‖X⊥TAX‖2. The result now follows directly from [22, Thm. 2] since A is real symmetric and the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 2(\lambda _1 - \lambda _n)$$\end{document}γ=2(λ1-λn). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

By the second-order Taylor expansion of f (see, e.g., [8], Corollary 10.54) it is easy to see that Proposition 1 implies10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X}) \le f(\mathcal {Y})+ \langle {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {Y}), {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {Y}} (\mathcal {X}) \rangle +\frac{\gamma }{2} {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {Y}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)≤f(Y)+⟨gradf(Y),LogY(X)⟩+γ2dist2(X,Y),for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}, \mathcal {Y} \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X,Y∈Gr(n,k) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}LogX(Y) is well-defined.

As in the introduction, denote the global minimum of f by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^*$$\end{document}f∗ which is attained at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\alpha \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}Vα∈Gr(n,k). Inequality (10) leads to the following lemma:

Lemma 4.2

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X} \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X∈Gr(n,k) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =2(\lambda _1-\lambda _n)$$\end{document}γ=2(λ1-λn), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X})-f^* \ge \frac{1}{2 \gamma } \Vert {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}) \Vert ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)-f∗≥12γ‖gradf(X)‖2.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^*$$\end{document}f∗ is a global minimum of f, we have from (10) that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f^* \le f(\mathcal {X}) \le f(\mathcal {Y})+\langle \text {grad}f(\mathcal {Y}),{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {Y}}(\mathcal {X}) \rangle +\frac{\gamma }{2} \Vert {{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {Y}}(\mathcal {X}) \Vert ^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f∗≤f(X)≤f(Y)+⟨gradf(Y),LogY(X)⟩+γ2‖LogY(X)‖2,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}, \mathcal {Y} \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X,Y∈Gr(n,k) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}LogX(Y) is well-defined.

We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}:={{\,\textrm{Exp}\,}}_{\mathcal {Y}} \left( -\frac{1}{\gamma } \text {grad}f(\mathcal {Y})\right) $$\end{document}X:=ExpY-1γgradf(Y). By Lemma 4.1, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\| -\frac{1}{\gamma } \text {grad}f(\mathcal {Y}) \right\| _2<\frac{\pi }{2}$$\end{document}-1γgradf(Y)2<π2 and by equation (5) we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {Y}}(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}LogY(X) is well-defined and equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\frac{1}{\gamma } \text {grad}f(\mathcal {Y})$$\end{document}-1γgradf(Y). Then,

the right hand side of the initial inequality becomes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f^* \le f(\mathcal {Y})-\frac{1}{\gamma } \Vert \text {grad}f(\mathcal {Y})\Vert ^2+ \frac{1}{2\gamma } \Vert \text {grad}f(\mathcal {Y})\Vert ^2 = f(\mathcal {Y})-\frac{1}{2 \gamma } \Vert \text {grad}f(\mathcal {Y})\Vert ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f∗≤f(Y)-1γ‖gradf(Y)‖2+12γ‖gradf(Y)‖2=f(Y)-12γ‖gradf(Y)‖2.Rearranging the last inequality and substituting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}=\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}Y=X, we get the desired result. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Weak-Quasi-Convexity and Quadratic Growth

We now turn our interest in the convexity properties of the block Rayleigh quotient function. We start by proving a property which is known in the literature as quadratic growth.

Proposition 2

(Quadratic growth) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \theta _1 \le \cdots \le \theta _k < \pi /2$$\end{document}0≤θ1≤⋯≤θk<π/2 be the principal angles between the subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\alpha $$\end{document}Vα. The function f satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\mathcal {X})-f^* \ge c_Q \, \delta \, {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {V_{\alpha }})$$\end{document}f(X)-f∗≥cQδdist2(X,Vα)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_Q = 4/\pi ^2 > 0.4$$\end{document}cQ=4/π2>0.4.

Proof

The spectral decomposition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = V_{\alpha } \Lambda _{\alpha }V_{\alpha }^T + V_{\beta } \Lambda _{\beta } V_{\beta }^T$$\end{document}A=VαΛαVαT+VβΛβVβT implies11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X^T AX = X^T V_{\alpha } \Lambda _{\alpha }V_{\alpha }^T X+ X^T V_{\beta } \Lambda _{\beta } V_{\beta }^T X. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}XTAX=XTVαΛαVαTX+XTVβΛβVβTX.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\mathcal {X}) = -{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(X^TAX)$$\end{document}f(X)=-Tr(XTAX), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X})-f^*&= {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\Lambda _{\alpha })-{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(X^T V_{\alpha } \Lambda _{\alpha }V_{\alpha }^T X)-{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(X^T V_{\beta } \Lambda _{\beta } V_{\beta }^T X) \\ &= {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\Lambda _{\alpha })-{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}( \Lambda _{\alpha }V_{\alpha }^T X X^T V_{\alpha })-{{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}( \Lambda _{\beta } V_{\beta }^T X X^T V_{\beta }) \\ &= {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\Lambda _{\alpha } (I_k- V_{\alpha }^T X X^T V_{\alpha })) - {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\Lambda _{\beta } V_{\beta }^T X X^T V_{\beta }). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)-f∗=Tr(Λα)-Tr(XTVαΛαVαTX)-Tr(XTVβΛβVβTX)=Tr(Λα)-Tr(ΛαVαTXXTVα)-Tr(ΛβVβTXXTVβ)=Tr(Λα(Ik-VαTXXTVα))-Tr(ΛβVβTXXTVβ).From the definition (7) of the principal angles between X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα, we recall that12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_{\alpha }^T X=U_1 \cos \theta \, V_1^T, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VαTX=U1cosθV1T,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cos \theta = {{\,\textrm{diag}\,}}(\cos \theta _1, \ldots , \cos \theta _k)$$\end{document}cosθ=diag(cosθ1,…,cosθk) is a diagonal matrix and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1, V_1$$\end{document}U1,V1 are orthogonal matrices. Plugging this equality in, we get that the jth eigenvalue of the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_k- V_{\alpha }^T X X^T V_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Ik-VαTXXTVα is equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\cos ^2\theta _j = \sin ^2 \theta _j \ge 0$$\end{document}1-cos2θj=sin2θj≥0. Thus, by standard trace inequality for symmetric and positive definite matrices (see, e.g., [18, Thm. 4.3.53]), the first summand above satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\Lambda _{\alpha } (I_k- V_{\alpha }^T X X^T V_{\alpha })) \ge \lambda _{k} \sum _{j=1}^k \sin ^2 \theta _j. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Tr(Λα(Ik-VαTXXTVα))≥λk∑j=1ksin2θj.The matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\beta }^T X X^T V_{\beta }$$\end{document}VβTXXTVβ has the same non-zero eigenvalues with the same multiplicity as the matrix\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X^T V_{\beta } V_{\beta }^T X = I_k-V_1 \cos ^2 \theta \, V_1^T = V_1 \sin ^2 \theta \, V_1^T \end{aligned}$$\end{document}XTVβVβTX=Ik-V1cos2θV1T=V1sin2θV1Twhere we used \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\beta } V_{\beta }^T= I_n - V_{\alpha } V_{\alpha }^T$$\end{document}VβVβT=In-VαVαT and the SVD of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\alpha }^T X$$\end{document}VαTX.
