Thus the jth eigenvalue of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\beta }^T X X^T V_{\beta }$$\end{document}VβTXXTVβ is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sin ^2 \theta _j \ge 0$$\end{document}sin2θj≥0. By trace inequality again, the second summand therefore satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Tr}\,}}(\Lambda _{\beta } V_{\beta }^T X X^T V_{\beta }) \le \lambda _{k+1} \sum _{j=1}^k \sin ^2 \theta _j. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Tr(ΛβVβTXXTVβ)≤λk+1∑j=1ksin2θj.Putting both bounds together, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X})-f^* \ge (\lambda _k - \lambda _{k+1}) \sum _{j=1}^k \sin ^2 \theta _j \ge \delta \sum _{j=1}^k \frac{4}{\pi ^2}\theta _j^2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)-f∗≥(λk-λk+1)∑j=1ksin2θj≥δ∑j=1k4π2θj2and the proof is complete by the definition (8) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}$$\end{document}dist. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We say that f is geodesically convex if for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}Y in a suitable region it holds\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f(\mathcal {X})-f(\mathcal {Y}) \le \langle \text {grad}f(\mathcal {X}), -{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {Y}) \rangle .$$\end{document}f(X)-f(Y)≤⟨gradf(X),-LogX(Y)⟩.This generalizes the classical convexity of differentiable functions on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn to manifolds by taking the logarithmic map instead of the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}-\mathcal {Y}$$\end{document}X-Y.

In Appendix A, we prove that our objective function f is only geodesically convex in a small neighbourhood of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {O}}(\sqrt{\delta })$$\end{document}O(δ) around the minimizer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\alpha $$\end{document}Vα. Fortunately, our key result of this section shows that f satisfies a much weaker notion of geodesic convexity, known in the literature as weak-quasi-convexity, that does not depend on the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ.

We first need the following lemma which is the general version of the CS decomposition but applied to our setting of square blocks.

Lemma 4.3

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{bmatrix} X&X_\perp \end{bmatrix}$$\end{document}XX⊥ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{bmatrix} Y&Y_\perp \end{bmatrix}$$\end{document}YY⊥ are orthogonal, the result follows directly from the CS decomposition of the orthogonal matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P = \begin{bmatrix} Y&Y_\perp \end{bmatrix}^T \begin{bmatrix} X&X_\perp \end{bmatrix}$$\end{document}P=YY⊥TXX⊥; see the Theorem of §4 in [29]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Observe that the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{diag}\,}}(I_r,C_s,O_{p \times p})$$\end{document}diag(Ir,Cs,Op×p) in this lemma corresponds to the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cos (\theta )$$\end{document}cos(θ) in (7) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ the vector of principal angles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \theta _1 \le \cdots \le \theta _k \le \pi /2$$\end{document}0≤θ1≤⋯≤θk≤π/2 between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(X)$$\end{document}span(X) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{span}\,}}(Y)$$\end{document}span(Y). However, the lemma explicitly splits off the angles that are zero and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi /2$$\end{document}π/2 so that it can formulate the related decompositions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^T X_\perp , Y_\perp ^T X,$$\end{document}YTX⊥,Y⊥TX, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_\perp ^T X_\perp $$\end{document}Y⊥TX⊥ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_s$$\end{document}Cs and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_s$$\end{document}Ss.

We are now ready to state our weak quasi-convexity result. In the statement of the proposition below (and throughout this paper), we use the convention that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{0}{\tan 0} = 1$$\end{document}0tan0=1.

Proposition 3

(Weak-quasi-convexity) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \theta _1 \le \cdots \le \theta _k < \pi /2$$\end{document}0≤θ1≤⋯≤θk<π/2 be the principal angles between the subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\alpha $$\end{document}Vα. Then, f satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 a(\mathcal {X}) \, (f(\mathcal {X})-f^*) \le \langle \text {grad}f(\mathcal {X}), -{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {V_{\alpha })} \rangle $$\end{document}2a(X)(f(X)-f∗)≤⟨gradf(X),-LogX(Vα)⟩with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(\mathcal {X}):= \theta _k / \tan \theta _k$$\end{document}a(X):=θk/tanθk.

\setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_\perp $$\end{document}X⊥ with orthonormal columns such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U_1^T \Lambda _\alpha U_1 - \tilde{U}_2^T \Lambda _\beta \tilde{U}_2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U1TΛαU1-U~2TΛβU~2is symmetric and positive semi-definite. Its diagonal entries, and thus also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{jj}$$\end{document}Sjj, are therefore nonnegative. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We now arrive at a useful property of f that will later allow us to analyze the convergence of Riemannian steepest descent. It is a weaker version of strong geodesic convexity and can be proved easily using quadratic growth and weak-quasi-convexity.

Theorem 4.1

(Weak-strong convexity) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \theta _1 \le \cdots \le \theta _k < \pi /2$$\end{document}0≤θ1≤⋯≤θk<π/2 be the principal angles between the subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\alpha $$\end{document}Vα. Then, f satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X})-f^* \le \frac{1}{a(\mathcal {X})} \langle {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}), -{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \rangle - c_Q \delta \, {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2 (\mathcal {X},\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)-f∗≤1a(X)⟨gradf(X),-LogX(Vα)⟩-cQδdist2(X,Vα)with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(\mathcal {X})= \theta _k / \tan \theta _k >0$$\end{document}a(X)=θk/tanθk>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_Q = 4/\pi ^2 > 0.4$$\end{document}cQ=4/π2>0.4, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta = \lambda _k - \lambda _{k+1} \ge 0$$\end{document}δ=λk-λk+1≥0.

Proof

Combining Propositions 2 and 3 leads to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c_Q \delta \, {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \le f(\mathcal {X})-f^* \le \frac{1}{2 a(\mathcal {X})} \langle {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}), -{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}cQδdist2(X,Vα)≤f(X)-f∗≤12a(X)⟨gradf(X),-LogX(Vα)⟩.At the same time, Proposition 3 also implies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X})-f^*&\le \frac{1}{2 a(\mathcal {X})} \langle {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}), -{{\,\textrm{Log}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}}(\mathcal {V}_\alpha ) \rangle - c_Q \delta \, {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {V}_\alpha ) \\ &\qquad + c_Q \delta \, {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {V}_\alpha ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(X)-f∗≤12a(X)⟨gradf(X),-LogX(Vα)⟩-cQδdist2(X,Vα)+cQδdist2(X,Vα).Using the first inequality to bound the last term of the right hand side, we recover the desired result. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 4.1

Theorem 4.1 is also valid when the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta = 0$$\end{document}δ=0. In that case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα is any subspace spanned by k leading eigenvectors of A and the theorem (almost) reduces to Proposition 3 (up to a scalar 2).

While not needed for our convergence proof, the next result is of independent interest and shows that f is gradient dominated in the Riemannian sense when the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ is strictly positive. This property is the Riemannian version of the Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequality and generalizes a result by [34] for the Rayleigh quotient on the sphere.

Proposition 4

(Gradient dominance) The function f satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \text {grad}f(\mathcal {X}) \Vert ^2 \ge 4 \, c_Q \, \delta \, a^2(\mathcal {X}) (f(\mathcal {X})-f^*) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖gradf(X)‖2≥4cQδa2(X)(f(X)-f∗)for all subspaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X that have a largest principal angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<\pi /2$$\end{document}<π/2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\alpha $$\end{document}Vα.

Proof

Convergence of Riemannian Steepest Descent

We now have everything in place to prove the convergence of the Riemannian steepest descent (RSD) method on the Grassmann manifold for minimizing f. Starting from a subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_0 \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}(n,k)$$\end{document}X0∈Gr(n,k), we iterate21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {X}_{t+1}={{\,\textrm{Exp}\,}}_{\mathcal {X}_t} (-\eta _t \, {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}f(\mathcal {X}_t) ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xt+1=ExpXt(-ηtgradf(Xt)).Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _t > 0$$\end{document}ηt>0 is a step size that may depend on the iteration t and will be carefully chosen depending on the specific case, but always depending on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ, which equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 (\lambda _1-\lambda _n)$$\end{document}2(λ1-λn).

We start by a general result which shows that the distance to the optimal subspace contracts after one step of steepest descent. The step size depends on the smoothness and weak-quasi-convexity constants of f from Propositions 1 and 3. This is crucial since the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(\mathcal {X})$$\end{document}a(X) depends on the biggest principal angle between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα and bounding the evolution of distances of the iterates to the minimizer will help us also bound this constant.5 An alternative contraction property with a more tractable step size is presented in Proposition 6 of Appendix B.

Lemma 5.1

(Contraction of RSD) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_t$$\end{document}Xt and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_\alpha $$\end{document}Vα have principal angles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \theta _1 \le \cdots \le \theta _k < \pi /2$$\end{document}0≤θ1≤⋯≤θk<π/2. Then, iteration (21) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \eta _t \le a(\mathcal {X}_t)/\gamma $$\end{document}0≤ηt≤a(Xt)/γ satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X}_{t+1},\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \le \big (1- 2 c_Q \delta a(\mathcal {X}_t) \, \eta _t \big ) {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X}_t,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dist2(Xt+1,Vα)≤(1-2cQδa(Xt)ηt)dist2(Xt,Vα).

Observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 0$$\end{document}γ=0 implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = \lambda _1 I$$\end{document}A=λ1I and any subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X of dimension k will be an eigenspace of A with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X},\mathcal {V}_\alpha )=0$$\end{document}dist(X,Vα)=0. We will therefore not explicitly prove this lemma and all forthcoming convergence results for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0 since the statements will be trivially true.

Proof of Lemma 5.1

By the assumption on the principal angles, we get that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< a(\mathcal {X}_t) = \theta _k / \tan \theta _k \le 1$$\end{document}0<a(Xt)=θk/tanθk≤1. Remark 5.1

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta =0$$\end{document}δ=0, Lemma 5.1 still holds for any subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα spanned by k leading eigenvectors of A. In that case, the lemma only guarantees that the distance between the iterates of steepest descent and this \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα does not increase.

dummy

Linear Convergence Rate Under Positive Eigengap

Lemma 5.1 features a contraction rate only for one step of the algorithm. In order to get a global convergence rate, one needs to bound the quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(\mathcal {X}_t)$$\end{document}a(Xt) from below and independently of t. To that end, we need a stricter bound in the distance of the initial guess to the optimum. Such a bound guarantees that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(X_t)$$\end{document}a(Xt) remains always lower bounded by a positive number, or equivalently, that the iterates of the algorithm never get too close to a non-optimal critical point.

Theorem 5.1

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) < \pi / 2$$\end{document}dist(X0,Vα)<π/2 then the iterates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_t$$\end{document}Xt of Riemannian steepest descent (21) with step size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _t$$\end{document}ηt such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0<\eta \le \eta _t \le \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) / \gamma \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<η≤ηt≤cos(dist(X0,Vα))/γsatisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {dist}^2(\mathcal {X}_t,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \le \left( 1- 2 c_Q \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))\, \delta \, \eta \right) ^ t {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dist2(Xt,Vα)≤1-2cQcos(dist(X0,Vα))δηtdist2(X0,Vα).

\end{aligned}$$\end{document}dist2(Xt+1,Vα)≤1-2cQa(Xt)δηtdist2(Xt,Vα).Combining with (23) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} If the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ is strictly positive, then Theorem 5.1 gives an exponential convergence rate towards the optimum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα. From Theorem 5.1 we get immediately the following iteration complexity.

Corollary 5.1

Let Riemannian steepest descent be started from a subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_0$$\end{document}X0 that satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) < \pi /2$$\end{document}dist(X0,Vα)<π/2 and with step-size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η satisfying the condition of Theorem 5.1. Then after at most\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T = 2 \frac{\log (\varepsilon ) - \log ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))}{\log (1- 0.8 \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) \delta \eta )} +1 \le {\mathcal {O}}\left( \frac{\log ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) - \log (\varepsilon )}{\cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) \delta \eta } \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T=2log(ε)-log(dist(X0,Vα))log(1-0.8cos(dist(X0,Vα))δη)+1≤Olog(dist(X0,Vα))-log(ε)cos(dist(X0,Vα))δηmany iterations, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_T$$\end{document}XT will satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_T,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \le \varepsilon $$\end{document}dist(XT,Vα)≤ε. With the maximal step size allowed in Theorem 5.1, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T \le {\mathcal {O}}\left( \frac{\lambda _1 - \lambda _n}{\delta } \frac{1}{\cos ^2({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))} \log \left( \frac{{{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })}{\varepsilon }\right) \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T≤Oλ1-λnδ1cos2(dist(X0,Vα))logdist(X0,Vα)ε.

Proof

In order to guarantee \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {dist}(\mathcal {X}_T,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \le \epsilon $$\end{document}dist(XT,Vα)≤ϵ, it suffices to have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( 1- 2 c_Q \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))\, \delta \, \eta \right) ^ T {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \le \epsilon ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1-2cQcos(dist(X0,Vα))δηTdist2(X0,Vα)≤ϵ2.Taking the logarithm of both sides, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T \log (1- 2 c_Q \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) \delta \eta ) + 2 \log ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) \le 2 \log (\epsilon ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Tlog(1-2cQcos(dist(X0,Vα))δη)+2log(dist(X0,Vα))≤2log(ϵ),which gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T \ge 2 \frac{ \log (\epsilon ) - \log ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))}{\log (1- 2 c_Q \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T≥2log(ϵ)-log(dist(X0,Vα))log(1-2cQcos(dist(X0,Vα)),since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log (1- 2 c_Q \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))$$\end{document}log(1-2cQcos(dist(X0,Vα)) is negative. By considering that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_Q \ge 0.8$$\end{document}cQ≥0.8, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T \ge 2 \frac{ \log (\epsilon ) - \log ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))}{\log (1- 0.8 \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) \delta \eta )}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T≥2log(ϵ)-log(dist(X0,Vα))log(1-0.8cos(dist(X0,Vα))δη),and the smallest integer that satisfies this inequality is exactly\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T = 2 \frac{ \log (\epsilon ) - \log ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))}{\log (1- 0.8 \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) \delta \eta )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T=2log(ϵ)-log(dist(X0,Vα))log(1-0.8cos(dist(X0,Vα))δη).The inequality part of the result follows by considering that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \log (1- 0.8 \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })\delta \eta ) \ge - \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) \delta \eta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}log(1-0.8cos(dist(X0,Vα)δη)≥-cos(dist(X0,Vα))δη.The final bound for T follows by a simple substitution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta =\cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) / \gamma $$\end{document}η=cos(dist(X0,Vα))/γ.

As expected, T depends inversely proportional on the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ and proportional to the spread of the eigenvalues. In addition, we also have an extra term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/\cos ^2({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))$$\end{document}1/cos2(dist(X0,Vα)) that depends on the initial distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })$$\end{document}dist(X0,Vα), which is due to the weak-quasi-convexity property of f. This is a conservative overestimation, since this quantity improves as the iterates get closer to the optimum.

Remark 5.2

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0, the exponential convergence rate is in terms of the intrinsic distance on the Grassmann manifold, that is, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ2 norm of the principal angles. Standard convergence results for subspace iteration are stated for the biggest principal angle, that is, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _\infty $$\end{document}ℓ∞ norm. This is weaker than the intrinsic distance. For subspace iteration with projection, the convergence result from [31, Thm. 5.2] shows that all principal angles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _i$$\end{document}θi converge to zero and eventually gives convergence of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _4$$\end{document}ℓ4 norm of the principal angles. This is also weaker than the intrinsic distance.

Convergence of Function Values Without an Eigengap Assumption

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta =0$$\end{document}δ=0, Theorem 5.1 still holds, but does not provide a rate of convergence as discussed above. Instead, we can prove the following result:

Theorem 5.2

If the distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })$$\end{document}dist(X0,Vα) of the initial subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_0$$\end{document}X0 to the minimizer satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })<\pi / 2$$\end{document}dist(X0,Vα)<π/2 for a subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα that is spanned by any k leading eigenvectors of A, then the iterates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_t$$\end{document}Xt of Riemannian steepest descent (21) with fixed step size\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta \le \cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })) / \gamma \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η≤cos(dist(X0,Vα))/γsatisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\mathcal {X}_t) - f^* \le \frac{2\gamma +\frac{1}{\eta }}{4 (\cos ({{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }))t+1)} {{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}^2(\mathcal {X}_0,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha })={\mathcal {O}}\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(Xt)-f∗≤2γ+1η4(cos(dist(X0,Vα))t+1)dist2(X0,Vα)=O1t.

Proof

Remark 5.3

This type of result is standard for functions that are geodesically convex (see, e.g. [35]). Our objective function does not satisfy this property, but we can still have a similar upper bound on the iteration complexity for convergence in function value. We note that this does not imply convergence of the iterates to a specific k-dimensional subspace, but only convergence of a subsequence of the sequence of the iterates.

Sufficiently Small Step Sizes

The convergence results in Theorems 5.1 and 5.2 require that the initial subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_0$$\end{document}X0 lies within a distance strictly less than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi /2$$\end{document}π/2 from a global minimizer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα. While this condition is independent from the eigengap (unlike results that rely on standard convexity, see appendix), it is also not fully satisfactory: it is hard to verify in practice, and it is unnecessarily severe in numerical experiments. In fact, this condition is only used to obtain a uniform lower bound on the weak-quasi-convexity constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(\mathcal {X}_t) = \theta _k^{(t)} / \tan (\theta _k^{(t)})$$\end{document}a(Xt)=θk(t)/tan(θk(t)) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k^{(t)}$$\end{document}θk(t) the largest principal angle between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_t$$\end{document}Xt and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα. Since the Riemannian distance is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ2 norm of the principal angles, a contraction in this distance leads automatically to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k^{(t)} < \pi /2$$\end{document}θk(t)<π/2 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k^{(0)} < \pi /2$$\end{document}θk(0)<π/2. If one could guarantee by some other reasoning that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k^{(t)} $$\end{document}θk(t) does not increase after one step, the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(\mathcal {X}_0, \mathcal {V}_\alpha ) < \pi /2$$\end{document}dist(X0,Vα)<π/2 would not be needed.

We now show that for sufficiently small step sizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _t$$\end{document}ηt, the largest principal angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k^{(t)} $$\end{document}θk(t) between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_t$$\end{document}Xt and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }$$\end{document}Vα does indeed not increase after each iteration of Riemannian steepest descent regardless of the initial subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_0$$\end{document}X0. While it does not explain what we observe in numerical experiments where large steps can be taken, it is a first result in explaining why we can initialize the iteration at a random initial subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_0$$\end{document}X0.

Proposition 5

Riemannian steepest descent started from a subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_t$$\end{document}Xt returns a subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_{t+1}$$\end{document}Xt+1 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _k(\mathcal {X}_{t+1},\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }) \le \theta _k(\mathcal {X}_t,\mathcal {V}_{\alpha }), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θk(Xt+1,Vα)≤θk(Xt,Vα),for all step sizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \eta \le \bar{\eta }$$\end{document}0≤η≤η¯ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bar{\eta }> 0$$\end{document}η¯>0 is sufficiently small.

For the proof of this proposition, we will need the derivatives of certain singular values. While this is well known for isolated singular values, it is possible to generalize to higher multiplicities as well by relaxing the ordering and sign of singular values [10]. For a concrete formula, we use the following result from Lemma A.5 in [24].

Lemma 5.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _1 \ge \cdots \ge \sigma _n$$\end{document}σ1≥⋯≥σn be the singular values of S \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\in \mathbb {R}^{n \times n}$$\end{document}∈Rn×n with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{1}, \ldots , u_{n}$$\end{document}u1,…,un and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{1}, \ldots , v_{n}$$\end{document}v1,…,vn the associated left and right orthonormal singular vectors. Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _j$$\end{document}σj has multiplicity m, that is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma _{j_0-1}> \sigma _{j_0} = \cdots = \sigma _j =\cdots = \sigma _{j_0+m-1} > \sigma _{j_0+m}. $$\end{document}σj0-1>σj0=⋯=σj=⋯=σj0+m-1>σj0+m.Then, the jth singular value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S+\eta $$\end{document}S+ηT satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _j(S+\eta T) = \sigma _j + \eta \lambda _{j - j_0 + 1} + {\mathcal {O}}(\eta ^2), \quad \eta \rightarrow 0^+, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σj(S+ηT)=σj+ηλj-j0+1+O(η2),η→0+,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{j}$$\end{document}λj is the jth largest eigenvalue of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{1}{2}(U^T B V + V^T B^T U)$$\end{document}12(UTBV+VTBTU) with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U = \begin{bmatrix} u_{j_0}&\cdots&u_{j_0+m-1} \end{bmatrix} \quad \text {and}\quad V = \begin{bmatrix} v_{j_0}&\cdots&v_{j_0+m-1} \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U=uj0⋯uj0+m-1andV=vj0⋯vj0+m-1.

Proof of Proposition 5

Lemma 5.2 thus gives that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _j(\eta ) \ge \sigma _j$$\end{document}σj(η)≥σj for sufficiently small and positive \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η. Numerical Experiment

We report on a small numerical experiment to verify the convergence rates proven above. The steepest descent iteration with fixed step size was implemented in Matlab using the geodesic formula (4).

As first test matrix, we took the standard 3D Laplacian on a unit cube, discretized with finite differences and zero Dirichlet boundary conditions. For completeness, we implemented steepest descent starting from a subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}_0$$\end{document}X0 far away from the optimum. However, a meaningful choice for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η is given by Proposition 7 of Appendix B, where we prove a local linear convergence rate for the function values of the iterates for step size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta =1 / \gamma $$\end{document}η=1/γ.

We see in Fig. 2 that despite the seemingly bad initial guess, steepest descent converges globally with a linear rate. The convergence in function value is visible in Fig. 3. Observe that we have displayed a logarithmic scale for both axes whereas before the figure had a logarithmic scale only for y-axis. Algebraic convergence like 1/t is therefore visible as a straight line. We see in the figure that the convergence is not easily described, and that there is no clear difference between zero or small gap. However, the upper bounds of Theorem 5.2 are again valid. In addition, when the gap is not small, the convergence is clearly faster.Fig. 3Steepest descent along geodesics for the block Rayleigh quotient of size k applied to a random matrix with small eigengaps. We observe again that the local nature of our theoretical results is quite pessimistic: the algorithm converges with an algebraic rate even with a bad initial guess but it shows eventually linear convergence.

Conclusion and Future Work

We provided the first non-asymptotic convergence rates for Riemannian steepest descent on the Grassmann manifold for computing a subspace spanned by k leading eigenvectors of a symmetric matrix A.

Our main idea was to exploit a convexity-like structure of the block Rayleigh quotient, which can be of much more general interest than for only analyzing steepest descent. One example is line search methods, which have usually favourable properties compared to vanilla steepest descent. Also, weakly-quasi-convex functions have been proven to admit accelerated algorithms [25], while accelerated or almost accelerated Riemannian algorithms have been developed in [5, 6, 36]. It would naturally be interesting to examine whether a provable accelerated method can be developed for the block Rayleigh quotient on the Grassmann manifold. This would hopefully reduce the dependence of the iteration complexity on the eigengap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {O}}(1/\delta )$$\end{document}O(1/δ) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {O}}(1/\sqrt{\delta })$$\end{document}O(1/δ).

Another interesting direction is to extend the analysis of [4] from the computation of just one leading eigenvector to computation of a whole subspace, using the generalized machinery developed in this work, or develop a noisy version of steepest descent and compare with noisy power method [14].
